1. Further to our telephone conversation today, I enclose a note
from APS/US of S covering a press cutting from the 5 January
edition of the Sunday Mirror alleging that the UK is developing a
Stealth aircraft. US of S has requested background information on
the article. ;

2. The note was sent to Sec(AS) because of our involvement in UFO/
Air defence matters. However, US of S's office subsequently
confirmed that the Minister's main interest is in the existence or
otherwise of a UK Stealth project and whether test flights are
being conducted. I should therefore be grateful if you could
provide the briefing requested.

3. Given this branch's involvement in UFO matters, and that trial
flights of a Stealth aircraft might account for some of the UFO
sightings reported to us, we would be interested in seeing a copy
of your advice to US of S. We would not, of course, use this
information in public correspondence.

Reference: S

A. D/Sec(AS)/64/1 dated 18 Dec 96.

1. You posed several questions in the Reference.

2. Although I could not remember any briefings by US agencies
during my considerable tenure in this office a search of all
existing UAP files showed no record of any meetings/briefings
with the CIA, or any other agency. Similarly we have no data on
the alleged "Roswell Incident" or any "crashed UFO incidents in
the UK".

3. In short DI 55 has no records of any UAP/UFO "crashes" in
either the UK or US and have never, a far as we can tell from
existing files, received and briefs from any US agencies,
including the CIA.

LOOSE MINUTE

D/DAO/1/13

Nov 96

Sec(AS)2al

UNUSUAL AIR PHENOMENON -_5 OCT 96

References:

A. D/Sec(AS)/64/1 dated 5 Nov 96.

B. D/DAOQ/i/13 dated 7 Oct 96.

Cc. D/DAO/1/13 dated 13 Nov 96.

Le At Reference A, you forwarded a minute from DI55¢ concerning

the unusual air phenomenon observed on 5 Oct 96, where the author
applied his own logic to an early report on the incident at
Reference B and provided his own personal observations.

2.

Much ink has already flowed on the events of the 5 Oct 96 and

the answers to DI55¢'s comments can all be found in the detailed
report at Reference C, however, I have extracted relevant notes
below in response to his specific points.

a. Para 2a. The Royal Greenwich Observatory states that
“when a bright star or planet is low down in the sky (even to
elevation 209), it is quite common for it to ‘show colours,
which are often described as flashing or rotating’. I do not
put much store by many of the estimates of elevation provided
by the observers; the more reliable Skegness video and the MV
CONOCOAST observations put the lights at 15-209 elevation.
Most significantly, the original observer watched the lights
all night and saw them become higher, dimmer and whiter until
“jit (became) obvious that it was just a star'.

b. Para 2b. The plot over Boston was only observed on one
radar and its characteristics confirm beyond reasonable doubt
that it was a permanent echo. It cannot be positively stated
that the “Boston Stump' (the 273ft spire of St Botolph's
church) was the cause without conducting further detailed
technical study, however, as the highest local physical
feature in the area. I can positively state that this theory
is highly probably.

c. Para 2c. Many of the “reported facts' have since proved
unreliable and a probable source of the lights has been
provided by the Royal Greenwich Observatory as the planet
Venus. There was no sign of air movements or military
exercises in the area and there was no evidence of
unidentified air activity on any radar, air defence or civil;
the separate stationary lights were not suspicious in nature
or of significance to air or maritime safety and are judged
to have been some form of natural phenomenon of no air
defence, air or even maritime concern. All the elevation
data on the lights, reliable or otherwise, confirms that, had
the lights been associated with a normal flying object, it
would have been within the coverage of at least 4 separate
radar heads unless advanced stealth or other technology was
being applied to remain electronically invisible; if a
penetrating aircraft or device was so equipped, however,
logic suggests that its crew would not have been so negligent
as to have left the lights switched on.

3. You may wish to send DI55c the attached copy of the Skegness
video; perhaps DIS can shed some “light' on the subject!

DI55e

UNUSUAL AIR PHENOMENON - 5 OCT 96
Reference: D/DI55/108/15 dated 1 Nov 96.

1. At Reference you forwarded your personal observations in
respect of early reports of the unusual aerial activity which was
seen during the early hours of 5 October in the area of The Wash.

2. You may be interested to know that following a Parliamentary
Enquiry received from Martin Redmond MP about these events, DAO's
organisation undertook a very thorough investigation including
interviewing key witnesses. This investigation was only
considered necessary in view of Mr Redmond's serious allegations
about the effectiveness of our Air Defence systems. In respect of
the three points you specifically make at reference you may be
interested in the following comments:

a. Para 2a. The Royal Greenwich Observatory states that
‘when a bright star or planet is low down in the sky (even to
elevation 209), it is quite common for it to show colours,
which are often described as flashing or rotating’. DAO
staff do not put much store by many of the estimates of
elevation provided by the observers; the more reliable
Skegness video and the MV CONOCOAST § maritime vessel)
observations put the lights at 15-209 elevation. Most
significantly, the original observer watched the lights all
night and saw them become higher, dimmer and whiter until ‘it
(became) obvious that it was just a star'.

b. Para 2b. The plot over Boston was only observed on one
radar and its characteristics confirm beyond reasonable doubt
that it was a permanent echo. It cannot be positively stated
that the ‘Boston Stump' (the 273ft spire of St Botolph's
church) was the cause without conducting further detailed
technical study, however, as the highest local physical
feature in the area. DAO staff believe that this theory is
highly probably. .

c. Para 2c. Many of the ‘reported facts' have since proved
unreliable and a probable source of the lights has been
provided by the Royal Greenwich Observatory as the planet
Venus which apparently had been exceptionally bright during
the week in question. There was no sign of air movements or
military exercises in the area and there was no evidence of
unidentified air activity on any radar, air defence or civil;

To CS(RM)
MOD INTEREST IN "UFO" SIGHTINGS

The Ministry of Defence has no interest, expertise or role with
respect to “UFO/flying saucer" matters, or the question of the
existence or otherwise of extraterrestrial lifeforms about which
we remain open-minded. To date, however, we remain unaware of any
evidence which proves that these phenomena exist.

The Ministry of Defence examines any reports of “UFO" sightings it
receives solely to establish whether what was seen might have some
defence significance; namely is there any evidence that the UK Air
Defence Region might have been compromised by a foreign hostile
military aircraft.

The reports are examined, with the assistance of the Department's
air defence experts as required. Unless there is evidence of a
potential military threat, and to date no "UFO" sighting has
revealed such evidence, we do not attempt to identify the precise
nature of each sighting reported to us. We could not justify
expenditure of public funds on investigations which go beyond our
specific defence remit. Wé believe that rational explanations

S NOTE

STANDARD REPLIES TO UFQ LETT! S OF IC

1. Head of Sec(AS) has instructed Sec(AS)2a that whereever
possible we should aim to reply to as many letters from the public
as we can with a two para response which gives a short statement
of the MOD's interest in this subject only. Clearly judgement
will need to be exercised to ensure that we do not breach the
terms of the Open Government initiative. However, Open Government
relates to our defence remit and much of the “UFO" subject falls
outside our defence remit. I shall draft a suitable template
shortly.

2. We have also been instructed that from now on we should not
give details of “UFO" organisations out to members of the public.
They should be advised to look through the range of "UFO"
magazines on sale at newsagents which contain details of a variety
of "UFO" organisations which have sprung up over the last few
years. No recommendations should be given. We know nothing about
any of them anyway, but we must not be seen to be steering people
in the direct of any particular organisation.

B IGHTINGS — S

All available sources of information have been examined and we are
satisfied that the sightings were not of air defence significance.
We are content that there was no associated threat to UK airspace.

IF_PRESSED:
THE RADAR RETURNS

The radar returns have been assessed and were almost certainly
caused by a permanent geographical feature (St Botolph's church
spire in Boston, 273ft). The church spire is known in aviation
circles as the 'Boston Stump’ and appears occasionally on some
radars in certain radar propagation conditions.

LIGHTS IN THE WASH AREA

The lights in The Wash area were observed from three separate
locations high above the horizon and in the same general
direction, but .without corroborating radar data. No associated
air vehicle was detected by civil or military radars. This
suggests a distant celestial source.

There were no corroborating radar returns for the visual
sightings.

AFDO

ADGEL
DI55C

REPORTING OF UFOs

1. Thank you for your minute of 19th September (AFDO/1/3/1) about
the reporting of 'UFOs'.

2. The Department's interest in 'UFO sightings' is one of air
defence, namely, whether there is any evidence in a sighting of a
breach of the UK air defences. Whilst this policy remains in
force Sec(AS)2, as the Air Staffs Secretariat, is required to
accept UFO reports from members of the public and to keep records
of all such reports where the witness is prepared to give his/her
name and contact details.

3. In looking at this issue it is important to remember that as
our declared interest is air defence, not ‘flying saucer’ related,
MOD must be seen to acknowledge at all times information provided
by members of the public about a sighting which might, in their
view, pose an air defence threat. We cannot expect the civil
police to decide what constitutes a potential threat nor who might
have information which the MOD might find of importance.

4. MOD and OGDs have to deal with Parliamentary Questions about
UFOs and also receive a significant number of letters from MPs
acting on behalf of their constituents about this issue. MOD
Ministers are required to respond to such inquiries and, in order
to staff these properly, Sec(AS)2 must have full and accurate
records of all reports made to the Department, however misguided
we might, at times, feel the informant to be.

5. It is simply not possible for Sec(AS)2 to provide round the
clock cover to respond to calls from members of the public about
low flying and UFOs and, as you know, out of hours this is done on
our behalf by the DCMC. However, I am unable to gauge the scale
of your own concerns about the number and content of the calls you
receive since, by your own admission, you do not report all of the
details on to us. For the last six months we have on record that
only 14 reports were forwarded to us by the DCMC. If you maintain
a logbook of all calls received it would be helpful to know the
details of all of those relating to UFOs which were not turned
into reports for, say, the last six months. From this we shall be
able to complete our records and assess by how much public
interest is increasing.
6. Once we have this sort of information we shall be better able
to look again at MOD policy on the handling of calls from the
public about UFo sightings. I should be grateful for your

assistanee.

Sec(AS)2

worth the telephone call involved and are not passed on to you via
formal report the following morning. Most importantly, however,
is the worrying notion that our telephone number seems to be
freely given by the police to anyone, at any hour of the day or
night, without any preliminary screening of callers. This is
especially irritating when one has just received the third
consecutive call from the same persistent ‘reporter' within 45

for one , have a strong feeling that the AFDO has more relevant
matters to deal with, and this will become even more pronounced in
the near future when we are ‘trans-muted’' into CDS. Duty staff.

MOD INTEREST IN “UFO” SIGHTINGS

The Ministry of Defence has no interest, expertise or role with
respect to “UFO/flying saucer” matters, or the question of the
existence or otherwise of extraterrestrial lifeforms about which
we remain open-minded. To date, however, we remain unaware of any
evidence which proves that these phenomena exist.

The Ministry of Defence examines any reports of "UFO" sightings it
receives solely to establish whether what was seen might have some
defence significance; namely is there any evidence that the UK Air
Defence Region might have been compromised by a foreign hostile
military aircraft?

The reports are examined, with the assistance of the Department's
air defence experts as required. Unless there is evidence of a
potential military threat, and to date no "UFO" sighting has
revealed such evidence, we do not attempt to identify the precise
nature of each sighting reported to us. We could not justify
expenditure of public funds on investigations which go beyond our
specific defence remit. We believe that rational explanations
could be found for the "UFO" sightings reported to the MOD if
resources were devoted to so doing. From the types of
descriptions we receive aircraft or natural phenomena probably
account for most of the observations.
August 3, 1996

Field Marshal Sir Peter Inge, QCB, ADCGen
Chief of the Defence Staff

UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECTS — POLICY

1. Following the rush of PQs generated by the publication of Mr
Pope's book, Head of Sec(AS) expressed his wish that we instigate
a formal procedure for logging the actions taken with respect to
each UFO sighting report received by the Department. This was
intended to ensure that a written record of any action taken is
instantly available in future should we be required to respond to
PQs or PEs.

2. We briefly discussed the resource implications of undertaking
a formal procedure in respect of each of the 300 odd reports we
receive annually. I expressed my concern that a closer
examination of the Department's policy in general is required, to
establish exactly what our role is and what we are under remit to
do with the reports we receive. I undertook to put together a few
bullet points briefly setting out how matters are currently
handled and my general thoughts on where we ought to be heading.
This I attach.

Enc.
Sec
ay)

Paris

3 _— MOD. ‘ag

Present Position S

- MOD policy is to examine and determine air defence
significance of reported UFO sightings.

- Sec(AS)2a role is to act as MOD focal point for:

— MOD Policy

-—- UFO reports

-- Parliamentary and Ministerial Business

— Public correspondence and telephone enquiries

- Press Office deals with enquiries from media, based on advice
from Sec(AS).

- DAO/GE1 advise Sec(AS) on air defence aspects, DI55 advise on
technology aspects.

Defence Interest?

- The DI55 remit is to look at sightings to determine whether
there is any technology operating in the UK of which, from an
intelligence perspective, we are not aware. DI55 has seen no
evidence which supports the extraterrestrial hypothesis.

- DAO has no interest in extraterrestrial matters. (They
concentrate their efforts on conventional military air
defence threats).

- Any extraterrestrial activity of defence significance, if it
ever occurred (whether military pilot reported, or detected
by air defence radar or early warning system) would be
handled by the Air Defence organisation. We might get to
hear about it sometime after the event.

- Possible defence interest might stem from sightings by
“credible witnesses", eg. professional civil pilots, ATCCs,
the emergency services (police/fire), although there is
currently no formal procedure for handling these within MOD.

-- On the two occasions in the last two years that civil
pilots have made airmiss reports, they have been
investigated by CAA/MOD Joint Airmiss Working Groups.
I passed details to D Air Def/GE3 who confirmed that
they were not aware of any evidence of a matter of
defence concern associated with the sightings.

- There. is a public perception (fuelled by my predecessor's
book) that MOD has a UFO investigation office. This

1

Sec(AS)2al is not resourced to provide an “aeronautical

flying objects. ‘Bees Tpstead Fesponses setting out MOD's role

Organisational Impact of (Policy Issues }

MOD's role would be reduced significantly, but more focused,

Sec(AS) would continue its focal point role - including
handling PQs/PEs - but focused solely on internally generated
(air defence) or "credible witness" reports which may have
some defence interest (currently amounting to a handful per
year).

DAO and DI55 must be fully involved in this process.

Sec(AS), DAO and DI55 would be involved in answering POs
and PEs relating to such reports.

Press Office would continue to handle any media
interest.

We would need to refresh UFO reporting procedures for
the CAA/NATS (from professional pilots or ATCCs), and
the emergency services (police/fire etc).

We would need to draw up a standard letter to the public
explaining why we no longer accept UFO reports from the

public and arrange for these to be despatched via DPR's

organisation (with the flyer if this idea is adopted).
lying Objects and Air Force Project Blue Book
rovorw dtio mil) gi-bin/waisgate?WAISdocID=89055773 7+0+0+0&WAISaction=retrieve Page 1

USAF Fact Sheet 95-03 :

Unidentified Flying Objects and Air Force Project Blue Book

From 1947 to 1969, the Air Force investigated Unidentified Flying Objects under Project Blue Book. The Project,
headquartered at Wright-Patterson Air Force Base, Ohio, was terminated Dec. 17, 1969. Of a total of 12,618 Sightings
reported to Project Biue Book, 701 remained “unidentified.”

The decision to discontinue UFO investigations was based on an evaluation of a report Prepared by the University of
Colorado entitled, "Scientific Study of Unidentified Flying Objects;" a review of tha University of Colorado's by the
Neon Academy of Sciences; previous UFO stadies and Air Force experience investigating UFO reports the 1940s,
"$08 and ‘60s.

As a reault of these investigations, studies and experience gained from investigating UFO reports since 1948, the conclusions
-of Project Blue Book were! (1) no UFO reported, investigated and evaluated by the Air Force was ever an indication of
threat to our national securit ; (2) there was no evidence submitted to or discovered by the Air Force that sightings
categorized as “unidentified” represented technological developments or principles beyond the Tange of modem scientific
knowledge; and (3) there was no evidence indicating that sightings categorized as "unidentified" were extraterrestrial

‘With the termination of Project Blue Book, the Air Force regulation establishing and controlling the ‘Program for
investigating and analyzing UFOs was rescinded, Documentation regarding the former Blue Book investigation was
permanently transferred to the Modern Military Branch, National Archives and Records Service, Eighth Street and
Pennsylvania Avenue, N,W., Washington, D.C. 20408, and is available for public review and analysis,

Since the termination of Project Blue Book, nothing has occurred that would support a resumption of UFO investigations by
the Air Force. Given the current environment of steadily decroasing defense budgets, it is unlikely the Air Force would
become involved in such a costly project in the foreseeable future.

There are a number of universities and professional scientific organizations that have considered UFO phenomena during
Periodic meetings and seminars. A list of private organizations interested in aerial phenomena may be found in Gale's
Encyclopedia of Associations, Interest in and timely review of UFO reports by private groups ensures that sound evidenco is
Not overlooked by the scientific community, Persons wishing to report UFO sightings should be advised to contact local law
enforcement agencies.

Point of Contact

Newa media requiring Project Blue Book files should contect the National Archives Public Affairs Office, (202)-501-5525,
Public queries should be addressed to the Project Blue Book archivist at (202) 501-5385. For queries not related to Project
Blue Book, contact the National Archives receptionist at (202) 501-3400.

(Current as of June 1995)

nature of each sighting reported to us. We could not justify
expenditure of public funds on investigations which go beyond our
specific defence remit. We believe that rational explanations
could be found for the “UFO" sightings reported to the MOD if
resources were devoted to so doing. From the types of
descriptions we receive aircraft or natural phenomena probably
account for most of the observations.

"UFO" reports from members of the public are generally made to
police stations, RAF bases, and civil air traffic control centres.
The reports are forwarded to the MOD focal point to be looked at.
Secretariat(Air Staff)2a, the single service secretariat branch
which provides administrative support for RAF activities and
operations, is the nominated MOD focal point for the subject of
"UFOs". Although there aré two members of staff in Sec(AS)2a who
deal with “UFO"-related queries received by the MOD as part of
their much wider ranging secretariat duties, there are no staff
who work on this topic full-time.

immediately identifiable to the witness. They should not be taken
to reflect sightings of "UFO/flying saucers".

OA LETTER/MENWITH SE 96 40
DRAFT REPLY FOR RAF CDR MENWITH HILL TO SEND 10 M0

Thank you for your letter dated 29 June concerning RAF Menwith

Hill.

RAF Menwith Hill is a Crown freehold site owned by the Ministry of
Defence and made available to the US Department of Defense as a
communications facility. The station is an integral part of the
US Department of Defense world wide network which supports UK, US
and NATO interests. It operates with the full knowledge and
consent of Her Majesty's Government and the function of the site
is regarded as being of the highest importance to this country's
defence strategy. Whilst the administration of the station is the
responsibility of the United States authorities, Her Majesty's
Government retains control over the use of the site and its

facilities.

As you will appreciate, for security reasons it is not the
Government's policy to comment on the detailed operations at RAF
Menwith Hill, however, I can assure you that the base has

absolutely no connection with Unidentified Flying Objects.

I hope you find the above helpful.

‘aly this month, an all
Ee gyoup of MPs voted

to cut the income support
of single mothers who refuse

‘Support Agency (CSA). From

like me wil live and raise

cut of £19.16 (40 per cent of

allowance). The MPs also

cause’ because they fear harm
or undue distress to their
families.

‘When the 1993 Act came.

and thelr children, even
‘though single mothers were
‘the first to oppose tt. Since

stopped claiming Income
‘support, Now, the higher
penalty aims to starve even
more single mothers of
benefits, forcing them either
back to men they don’t want to
be with or into destitution,
prostitution or Jobs paying
under £57 a week.

‘Single mothers involved in
the Campaign Against the Child
Support Act (CACSA) are
‘tained to give accurate and
Independent advice. Every day
we speak to others Ike
ourselves, who have reasons
for not co-operating which the

get advice untit the benefit

deduction is in place because

Many women
and hard to leave violent

INVITATION TO APPEAR ON LOCAL RADIO TO DISCUSS UFOs

Reference: RAF Valley fax of 6 Oct 94,

1. We hav. idered the invitation you have received to appear on
che RSG Show on Marcher Coast FM and believe the invitation
shou e-deciined. I would like to explain clearly why we have
reached this judgement, and how best you might respond to the radio
station.

2. The scope of your role during this programme would be to
represent the MOD's official policy and line towards the subject of
unexplained aerial sightings. We have found that there is widespread
misunderstanding amongst members of the public, and indeed amongst
some MOD/RAF officials, as to precisely what that role is.

3. | MOD interest is concerned solely with whether an unexplained
aerial incident represents a threat to the UK's air defences. If we
find no evidence of such a threat we make no effort to investigate
what may have been seen, and our interest thus terminates. To date we
have received no evidence whatsoever to substantiate the existence of
any "UFOs" in the commonly accepted meaning of the acronym; ie. flying
saucers with aliens aboard. We remain open-minded about those
sightings which are not easily explained, as it is beyond our remit to
investigate further.

4. During the course of this programme, as a serving officer
representing the RAF/MOD, you would not be able to speculate or offer
any personal opinions. It would be very difficult to sustain the MOD
line, (which would take about one minute at most to deliver), faced
with the pressure to say something interesting. It is highly likely
you would be asked about your own personal views and about what course
of action we would take if we received reliable evidence of extra~
terrestrial aggression. This would be impossible to answer, for the
appropriate response would, of course, be determined in the light of
the particular circumstances. But any speculation or your part risks
being run back at us as a statement of UK MOD policy.
5. The result would almost certainly make for an uninspiring
programme and may fuel the view that we are actually hiding something.
Contrary to your assertion about possible PR value to Valley and the
RAF, we believe the opposite to be more likely.

6. Therefore we would suggest that you respond to the invitation in
the following way:

MOD has a specific and limited interest in unexplained
aerial sightings: we look at sightings to identify whether
they represent any threat and if they do not then that is
the end of our interest.

MOD's role is to maintain the security of the UK and to
protect against any aggressor and unless a potential threat
is thought to exist we do not try to determine what may have
been seen. We do not analyse or undertake any research into
unusual aerial sights which represent no threat.

Role of Valley CRO is to put forward the RAF/MOD position,
which it would take one minute to express. As a
representative of MOD, unable to speculate beyond the formal
position or offer any personal opinions, even if it were
stressed these were only personal opinions.

Do not therefore believe there is any useful contribution
that I/RAF Valley could make to this programme.

7. I have discussed the above with the RAF Press Office who agree

our line.

I am sorry if the above is a disappointing response.

Should you wish to discuss this matter further please give me a call.

Copy to:

DPO

Yours sincerely,

Unidentified Flying Objects and Air Force Project Blue Book
http://www.dtic.mil/ogi-bin/waisgate?WAISdocID=89055773 7+0+0+0& WA ISaction=retrieve Page 1

USAF Fact Sheet 95-03
Unidentified Flying Objects and Air Force Project Blue Book

From 1947 to 1969, the Air Force investigated Unidentified Flying Objects under Project Blue Book. The project,
headquartered at Wright-Patterson Air Force Base, Ohio, was terminated Dec. 17, 1969, Of total of 12,618 sightings
reported to Project Biue Book, 701 retained "unidentified."

The decision to discontinue UFO investigations was based on an evaluation of a teport prepared by the University of
Colorado entitled, "Scientific Study Of Unidentified Flying Objects;" a review of the University of Colotado's report by the
National Academy of Sciences; previous UFO studies and Air Force experience investigating UFO reports during the 1940s,
"50s and ‘60s.

AS areault of these investigations, studies and experience gained from investigating UFO reports since 1948, the conclusions
of Project Blue Book were: (1) no UFO reported, investigated and evaluated by the Air Force was ever an indication of
threat to our national security; (2) there was no evidence submitted to or discovered by the Air Force that sj is

categorized as “unidentified” represented technological developments or principles beyond the range of modem scientific
knowledge; and (3) there was no evidence indicating that sightings categorized a “unidentified” were extraterrestrial
vehicles.

With the termination of Project Blue Book, the Air Force teguiation establishing and controlling the program for
investigating and analyzing UFOs was rescinded, Documentation regarding the former Blue Book investigation was
permanentiy transferred to the Modem Military Branch, National Archives and Records Service, Eighth Srrset and
Pennsylvania Avenue, N.W., Washington, D.C. 20408, and is available for public review and analysis,

Since the termination of Project Blue Book, nothing has occurred that would support a resumption of UFO investigations
the Air Force. Given the current environment of steadily decreasing defense budgets, it is unlikely the Air Foree would
become involved in such a costly Project in the foreseeable furure,

‘There are a number of universities and professional scientific organizations that have considered UFO phenomena. during
periodic meetings and seminars. A list of private organizations interested in aerial phenomena may be found in Gale's
Encyclopedia of Associations, Interest in and timely review of URO reports by private groups ensures that sound evidence is
not overlooked by the scientific community. Peraons wishing to report UFO sightings should be advised to contact local law
eaforcement agencies.

Point of Contact

‘News media requiring Project Blue Book files should contact the National Archives Public Affairs Office, (202)-501-5525,
Public queries should be addressed to the Project Blue Book archivist at (202) 501-5385, For queries not related to Project
Blue Book, contact the National Archives receptionist at (202) 501-400.

(Current as of June 1995)

THE INDEPENDENY SECTION TWO » MONDAY 3 JUNE 1996,
s

1 most families it is parents who take care of the
children and only old age reverses the roles. But
for some children the reversal comes much
sooner as physical or mental health problems
overtake their mum or their dad and there is no
one else to take the strain. As many as 40,000
children may be fulfilling the roles of-little mothers
and fathers, according to a new report’, and one Chil-
dren’s Society report found children as young as three
suffering anxiety and stress as a result of caring.

Children lose out on their education, their friends
and their freedom as they shoulder practical burdens
such as the shopping and housework or the psycho-
logical ones of pain, illness or erratic behaviour,

Eltie Davey of Winchester was 11 years old when
her mother Ann, 43, was diagnosed as having mul-
tipic sclerosis, Now 16, Ellic takes on much of the
day to day care for her mother at a time when most
other tecnagers are still free of the cares and respon-
sibilitics that will catch up with them soon enough.
« Hec sister, Charlotte, who used to be able to help
has now gone to university in London and her step-
father, Chris, works fong hours as a school teacher
and is not back until late.

‘So when Ellie comes home there is dinner to cook,
homework to do, dirty clothes that need washing and
sometimes tears of pain from her mother lying under
a blanket on the sofa. There is no slumping in front
of Home and Away with a cup of tea and a biscuit.

Her school work has already suffered. Shc has got
nine GCSEs but al grades she knows are beneath her
ability. “When I was studying for my GCSEs, I had
tolet a lot of my schoolwork go. By the time I'd made
dinner and cleared up and maybe hung up the wash-
ing, I was too tired,” she says.

“There was a time when mum was in hospital for
eight weeks when I got quite depressed. 1 just didn't
fee! like doing school work. Seeing your mum in such

pain makes you fee! so sad, When I didn’t do my essays,
Paget into trouble at school. Some of my teachers
warmed me if I didn’t work Pd fail my exams. They
didn’t support me by telling mc the most essential work
Tcould have done. They just thought I was lazy.”

‘She has lost friends, too. Her best friend was not
very understanding when her mother fell ill so they

are no longer close. “You really find out who your
friends are,” she says. “You can confide in some of
them, but when you're a teenager, it’s just not cool
to have a mother in a wheelchair. No one wants to
hear about your sick mum all the time.”

‘Yet she is not resentful. “At first you think cvery-
thing is so unfair. But you can’t spend your life being
bitter, you have to come to terms with it. What E think
is more difficult is the psychological side of things.
If mum’s crying it’s really hard.”

One positive aspect is that she feels she and her
mum have avery close personal relationship ~- they
are more like friends than mother and daughter.
She has also learnt new skills by looking after her
mother and is more patient too. “But life is very

‘You really find out who your friends
are. You can confide in some of them,
but when you're a teenager, it’s just not
cool to have a mother in a wheelchair’

routine and boring,” she says.

Since she first tell ill in 1930. Ann’s condition has
gradually deteriorated, making most physical activ-
ity such as shopping, cooking and housework impos-
sible. Five ycars ago she juggled her job as a nurséry
nurse with running the house and bringing up two
daughters. Now she is confined to the sofa or a wheel-
chair for much of the time, sometimes in excruciat-
ing pain. She is decply frustrated by her lot.

“still fcc] guilty about my daughters missiag oul
on normal teenage years, but I now realise there’s
nothing I can do. You have to come to terms with it
otherwise you would become bitter and depressed,
and would only end up alienating your family.”

She was a vegetarian who enjoyed cooking a vari-
ety of dishes. Now she is grateful for whatever Ellie
can do. “It’s amazing the little things you miss when
you aren’t able to do them,” said Ann. “I still like to
cook but I have to get Ellie to cut up the vegetables
because my arms are too weak.”

Her health fluctuates. On some days she can walk

What does 16-year-old
Ellie do after school?

... She looks after her mother, who has multiple sclerosis —
she is one of thousands of child carers coping with
responsibilities way beyond their years. By Jo Kearney

for five to 10 minutes before her legs turn to jelly and
she becomes exhausted; at other times she sleeps for
wecks at a time. At times the pain has been so bad
she has had to spend long periods in hospitat.

“We all hate the illness because it prevents us lead-
ing a normal life,” she says. “But we have had to
adapt.”

Ellic was 11 when the words MS were first men-
tioned. “I'd heard of the illness because when I was
at primary schoo! we had a sponsored run to raise
money for the mother of one of the pupils. T always
thought it would he terrible to get something like
that,

“When mum was diagnosed I was numb. I was
depressed all the time, { couldn't concentrate on
lessons. My school friends were worrying about home-
work and I was thinking my mother might die. Now
my life is so strange and my priorities are different.
My friends might be concerned about buying the lat-
est bag or fashion, but that’s not important to me.

“1 go to friends’ houses and some of them have par.
ents who make their beds and even serve them meals
on a tray. After school my friends can go out, and
on Friday nights they hang around town and go out
to pubs. [ usually go home to care for mum, and any-
way I’m so tired a lot of the time.”

Ann _ desperately docs not want Ellie to miss out
ona social life but concedes it is very difficult for her
to have one because her mum cannot drive her to
the pictures and it is often difficult to bring friends
back home because of her mum’s condition.

Ann admits she would be lost without her daugh-
ters but she is adamant that they also have their own
lives and she is keen that Filie will go to university
as well as Charlotte even if it means her leaving
Hampshire, “I will bave to rely on the MS support
group and Chris,” she says.

Ellic says she would prefer not to go too far away.
“I know if I phone Mum she will say everything is

_ OK - even ifit is not.”

George,

feel like crying.

When T cut my throat, Billi found out several
months later, and when she asked Daddy about it,

say that about me. God, that hurts even now. 1 don’t

maybe that’s why I tolerated Malcolm's drinking for

Darling,

... We've had a High Court judge and his clerk visit
the wing this evening - Judge Ottley, I believe his
name was!

T looked for him, but too late, he had gone. I
wanted to say, ‘If you want to sce a life sentence being
carried out, why not go to our home and see the chil-
dren, broken by untimely separation from their
mother? Why not come at visiting times and see
screaming young children being dragged out after
their one and a half hours every fourteen days with
mum — whilsi inmates weep and officers block their
ears? Why not come after 8p.m. and tiptoe round the
landings to hear women cry tears of guilt and agony
alone behind locked doors?” Because he doesn’t want
the truth, he wants the platitudes he delivers, and
receives trom us! I was so cross with myself at miss-
ing this golden opportunity that I sat and wrote a furi-
ous letter to him.

I wish I could have shouted what I’ve written above
from the second or third landing. The effect would
have been electric — and would in all probability have
started other women off. Instead, like justice itself —
it was all a charade! No doubt he now feels, after one
hour in H-wing, well qualified to dish out life sen-
tences left, right and centre.

Tve decided that on domestic visits, if you talk too
much Pll just kick you, effectively shutting us both up
and affording us a little bit of silence... When I get
out, you can kiss all my other moles! It's 12.25 am.

All my love, Sara xx

‘THE INDEPENDENT SECTION TWO © MONDAY 3 JUNE 1996

Now I’m a believer

here is a sober-suited government official who

investigates every reported UFO sighting,

about 250 a year, and responds to cach with a

letter explaining, more often than not, that the

suspected Martian craft seen hovering in the
skies over some suburban back garden was in fact a
weather balloon, an unusual cloud, or an aeroplane
reflecting light in a peculiar way.

The Ministry of Defence post of Secretariat (Air
Staff) 2a-- otherwise known as the UFO desk —
the perfect job for a prosaic pedant who takes plea-
sure in pouring cold water on excitement about the
paranormal and the unexplained. Perhaps that's the
sort of person Nick Pope was when he was assigned
to the post five years ago. But he bas traversed gatax-
ies since then. “I came in as. sceptic and I came out
as a believer,” he says today as he launches his book
Open Skies, Closed Minds, A Government UFO
Expert Speaks Qut.

Pope has gone from being the MoD’ official “Fly-
ing saucers! Don't be silly!” man, to a leading har-
binger of the extra-terrestrial threat. He believes in
the probable existence of extra-terrestrial life forms
and that they visit Earth by stealth in spacecraft whose
technology far outstrips our own. He is now being
dubbed “the real Fox Mulder” after the raincoated
sleuth who investigates unexplained phenomena in
the cult TV series The X Files. His book is being billed
as the MoD’s “real X Files”.

‘When he joined the civil service a decade ago, he
naturally assumed that he would be “writing reports
and pushing papers around”. The job suited him.
Everything about Pope is sensible, cautious even,
from his dark jacket and moderate tie to the way he
speaks. His sentences are all neat and ordered and
whatever he says comes out sounding like a well-
rehearsed briefing. He also seems quite a shy and pri-
vate man, The only time he falters is when asked his
age. First he says 31, then he says 30.

"After a variety of MoD postings, including a stint
working on RAF training policy and another at the
joint operation centre during the Gulf War, Pope was
‘assigned in 1991 to the UFO desk — a post neither
he nor most of bis colleagues even knew existed.

Superficially, Pope’s life remained unchanged. As
before, each morning he filed with thousands of other
government bureaucrats into the vast, expressionless
MoD building. Hurrying down its labyrinthine cor-
ridors Pope would be seated at his desk by 9am. But
from one minute past nine through to Spm, he found
himself in what amounted to a parallel universe.

Nick Pope’s job at
the MoD was to pour
cold water on UFO
sightings. But the
one-time sceptic has
seen new light.

By Emma Brooker

“People came straight through to me on my direct,
line, so my phone could ring and it could be a mem-
ber of the public on the other end saying ‘I saw a UFO
last night.’ Indeed that happened on a daily basis,”
Pope explains. “There was a regular stable of ‘inter-
esting’ characters who would phone up often, I got
to know them all,” he says with a reserved smile.

“[ took a healthily sceptical attitude to the people
phoning in. Ninety-five per cent of all these sightings
do have conventional explanations, so it's very
important to keep your feet on the ground when
investigating cases,” but Pope never dismissed any
callers. “I interpreted John Major's Citizen Charter
ina full and liberal way and felt that if people wanted
to talk to me, even if their views were very off-beat,

T wou)d_give them a fair crack of the whip.”
ore he listened, the more Pope was
draw hat he now calls The UFO Mystery. “I

found fat I was talking to witnesses who claimed
they'd been abducted by aliens ... people who were
certainly traumatised by their experience ... and I was
having to impound radar tapes from the RAF to see
if we could track any of these UFOs.”

Pope’s scepticism was being gradually eroded by
“persuasive” reports, particularly from RAF person-
nel. The turning point - when he actually started to
believe in the existence of intelligent life “out there”
—came in 1993, “There
says, a glint appearing in
were besieged with call
detailed investigation. We had clear evidence that 4
structured craft breached the UK air defences. It was
seen by dozens of witnesses all over the country. Many
of them were police officers and military personnel.”

a word of devionls F mutents in sec(4s) .

After weeks of exhaustive-rescarch, Pope, in his
capacity as the Government’s UFO expert, reached
his carefully considered conclusion. “We checked for

. aircraft movements, but there was absolutely noth-

ing up at the time. [ think it was an extra-terrestrial
craft,” he declares,

Pope started to talk to leading ufologists and
groups such as Contact International and
BOFURA., Overcoming their initial hostility and
suspicion, Pope cultivated good relations with the
UFO lobby and regularly exchanged information on
sightings. But Pope found that the more diligently
he investigated unexplained sightings, the more
uncomfortable his bosses within the MoD became,
“T was never actively blocked by my superiors,” he
writes in his book, “but there were times when things
were made difficult for me; times when I was quite
deliberately given onc-cff tasks to divert me from
a UFO case.”

At this point, the American series, The X Files,
started appearing on British TV. By some uncanny
doppelganger eficct - the sort of phenomenon which
would take up a whole. Files episode - Pope seemed
to be turning into its lead character, Fox Mulder.
‘Their blandly pleasant looks are not dissimilar. The
faintly geckish, earnest manner is familiar. As Pope
walked down the corridors of the MoD, people would
whistle the programme’s haunting theme tune. He
even acquired Mulder’s nickname, “Spooky”. Both
Pope and Mulder certainly subscribed to the same
motto, The Truth is Out There.

“Yes, I do identify with him,” says Pope. “I devel-
oped a reputation as a maverick within the ministry
as throughout my three-year posting I was increas-
ingly persuaded by the evidence.” His promotion out
of the UFO job after three years into the financial
planning department, is viewed by some ufologists
as evidence of a government cover-up.

Pope dismisscs such rumours, but admits that alot
of the changes he made on the UFO desk are being
“quietly undone” by his successor. Certainly there was
a lot of resistance among some of his former bosses
to the publication of his book. “I took the view that
if the generals could write their books on the Gulf
War, then I would write this book. I think the pub-
lic has a right to know what’s going on,” says Pope.

Which is what? Sadly, Pope himself has never seen
a UFO or had a close encounter (“I live in hope,”
he grins). Still, he has no conclusive evidence that
there is intelligent life in outer space. The real X files
contain no revelations, Pope's book, which has had

Saturday papers, The cappuccino comes
and then is taken away again because it
was someone else’s. Another is put in its
place.

I read an article by a woman called
Marian Partington, whose younger sister
was a victim of the Wests. I begin read-
ing it because it's there, but by the end I
am daunted, moved, altered, bereaved.

RESPONSES TO OU! =

“UNEXP: “

Many of the questions asked are based on a misunderstanding of the
MOD's role in respect of this subject, which has previously been
explained. However, the following are the answers we are able to
give to the questions as posed.

(1) See attached.

(2) Specific records are not kept. The vast majority of
“unexplained" aerial sightings reported to the MOD are ground
observations made by members of the. public.

(3)(4)(5) The MOD and HM Forces have responsibility for the
effective defence of the United Kingdom and we remain vigilant for
any potential threat, from whatever source. It is in this context
alone that we look at reports of "unexplained" aerial sightings in
order to establish whether what was seen might have defence
significance. If there is no evidence to suggest that the UK's
air defences have been compromised we do not investigate or seek
to establish the precise nature of what was observed. As such it
is quite common for a sighting to remain "unexplained" but not
require any further MOD action. The Royal Air Force continually
polices the UK Air Defence Region to ensure that no unauthorised
or hostile military aircraft enters UK airspace. We would look
for evidence that the UK Air Defence Region has been compromised.

(6) The UK MOD does not and has not undertaken research into the
nature of the "“UFO/flying saucer" phenomenon, either alone or with
other Governments.

(7)(8)(16)(17) In accordance with the provisions of the Public
Records Act of 1958 and 1967 all surviving paperwork from over
30 years ago on the subject of "UFOs" previously held by the MOD
has now been transferred to the Public Records Office and is
available for public examination,

(9) See answer to Questions 3, 4 and 5.

(10) Records of the amount of staff time spend on examination of
reports of “unexplained" aerial sightings are not maintained, but
the sums involved would represent a tiny fraction of the figure
quoted.

(11) Secretariat(Air Staff)2a is a MOD civilian secretariat branch
which provides administrative support for RAF activities and
operations, As part of its much wider ranging secretariat duties,
Sec(AS)2a is the focal point for handling any "UFO" reports
received by the MOD. As part of the assessment of reports
Sec(AS)2a staff consult relevant Departmental air defence experts
as required.
(12)(13)(14). The vast majority of "UFO" sightings are reported by
members of the public. They are made to a variety of
organisations eg. RAF stations, air traffic control centres at
civil airports and civil police stations, as well as to the MOD
directly. All reports are passed to Sec(AS)2a for examination.
Prior to 1992 any reports from members of the public received by
RAF stations were, in the first instance, forwarded to the Flying
Complaints Flight at RAF Rudloe Manor. The FCF acted as the RAF‘s
coordination point for passing such reports to the MOD. The FCF
took no action on the reports received and simply forwarded them
to Sec(AS)2a. Since 1992 the FCF's ‘post-box' role has been
dispensed with.

(15) Cannot answer tor Mie 40

(18) There were three other sightings reported to the MOD for
2 Feb 96 not four as previously stated. The fourth mentioned
earlier was infact observed on 1 Feb 96.

Descriptions of other reports for 2 Feb 96:

Location Description Given by Witness
(1) Clydebank White light with blue/purple. Like a
bright star - looked solid through
binoculars.

(2) M3, just past Cube-like in shape although the top
M25 junction was bigger than the bottom giving it
a ‘metal cup cake' appearance. Object
had a reddish glow to it and was the size
of ‘several detached houses'.

(3) Pitlessie One mushroom shaped brilliant white
object with red and blue flashing lights.

(19) The Ministry of Defence does not have a role with respect to
“UFO/flying saucer" or extraterrestrial matters. Our interest in
reports of "unexplained" aerial sightings received relates solely
to whether there is any evidence that the UK air defence region
has been compromised. We keep an open mind about the existence of
extraterrestrial life, but to date we know of nothing which proves
that this alleged phenomenon exists. Against this background, it
is felt that a MOD spokesperson would have little to contribute to
a programme on this subject.

INED AERIAL PHENO!

1. One of the roles of Sec(AS)2 is to act as focal point, within
UK Central Government, for reports of unexplained aerial phenomena
(commonly known as "UFO" reports, although the latter has
connotations which we do not seek to encourage). There is a need
for such a focal point since members of the public from time to
time wish to report sights they have seen in the sky and have been
unable to explain. In addition Government departments, and
Ministers, receive a steady stream of letters from the UFO lobby.
There is a need to deal with and providé responses to such
approaches.

2. The task falls to Sec(AS) because the official interest in
"UFO sightings" is an air defence one: ie. is there any evidence
in a sighting of a breach of UK air defences? our role in
relation to reports is therefore to examine them, with the
assistance of the appropriate experts, to ascertain whether the
sighting represents anything of defence interest. If we judge
that it does not, and this has been the case in respect of every
report to date, we do not seek to investigate further or to
provide an explanation of what might have been seen. In addition
we undertake the normal secretariat functions in drafting
responses to letters from the public.

3. Not surprisingly some of the letters we receive from the UFO
lobby seek to explain sightings in terms of extraterrestrial
craft, and some allege an official cover-up of evidence of
extraterrestrial life. We respond to such letters by stating that
the MOD has no role or expertise in such matters and that we
retain an open mind on them, although we are not to date aware of
any evidence to substantiate the existence of extraterrestrial
craft or beings.
4. Although Sec(AS) has had this responsibility for many years

@ row, our role has recently attracted a degree of publicity, not
least because of the activities of a former member of the division
who has written a book on the topic. Our role has therefore come
to the attention of senior management, and 2nd PUS has asked how
our approach to these matters compares to that of the US
Government. Hence this letter.

5. I would be grateful if you could ascertain what the US
approach to these matters is. I would stress that we are not
specifically concerned with major studies into the UFO phenomenon.
We are aware that the US authorities have conducted such studies
in the past but to the best of our knowledge have none underway at
present (if we are wrong on this point correction would be
appreciated). Rather we are interested to know what arrangements
are in place to deal with the inevitable flow of routine UFO
sightings and correspondence from the UFO lobby. Answers to the
following questions would therefore be appreciated:

a. Are there any central arrangements in place at Federal
Government level, within the DoD or elsewhere, to deal with
reports and enquiries about UFOs? If not, who, if anyone,
deals with them?

b. What is the extent of official interest in these
matters? Does it extend beyond (or even as far as) the
defence interest in sightings, plus the need to handle
incoming correspondence?

Cc. What is the official line on claims of extraterrestrial
involvement, and of an official cover-up?

6. I would be grateful for an early response, If you need more
information please contact me, or con 1) my staff (extn
82140MB) who deals with the "UFO" -

Yours auvearedy

AS FOR the UFO-related theories, I
could not give much credence to the
idea originating in Queensland, Aus-
tralia, that the circles were' UFO
landing nests.

‘The saucers would be weird indeed
if the pictograms were their imprint
in the corn. But as far as I was con-
cerned, that was not the end of the
UFO possibilities, Far from it.

‘The designs could certainly still be
extra-terrestrial in origin, perhaps
made by an energy beam or remote-
controlled probe. Again, assuminy
the existence of alien beings an
“heir ability to reach us, the ereation

‘fol and mysterious pat

circles, this explanation goes, is to
communicate with us, just as early
humans did with ‘each other
through pictograms and symbols,
To ensure smooth storylines and
familiarity among readers and film-
goers, science fiction has conven-
jently assumed that aliens can
speak our languages, but we need
not be so naive.

Indeed, official attempts are now
being made to contact other civili-
sations in our universe. NASA, for
example, transmits radio signals,
unmistakably artificial ones such as
sequences of prime numbers, so
that alien cultures cannot assume
they are natural in_ configuration,
and binary code sequences which,
when decoded into black and white
components, take on the crude fig-
ure of a human being. The frequen-
cies are chosen to avoid confusion
with signals naturally emitted by
pulsars and other stellar objects.

Perhaps someone or something 1s
using our flelds in the same way In
an attempt to contact us. The corn
is a blank canvas. Etch on it pat-
‘erns which ere unmistakably artifi-

‘and, as humans attempt to

explain them away naturally or sci-
entifically, make them more and
more complex so that they cannot
be dismissed as either plasma vorti-
ces or hoaxes,

‘Mathematics, it is said, is the only
universal language. If this is so, is it,
possible that extra-terrestrials
would use a form of mathematics to
communicate with us?

Benoit Mandelbrot devised a
shape on an IBM computer as a
representation of the concept, of
infinity. An exact replica of that
shape appeared in a corn field in
England in 1993, Coincidence? A
hoax by a team of university profes-
sors? Or something less earthly?

‘And what of the quintuplets, one
large circle surrounded by ‘four
smaller ones? Could the message be
as simple as ‘We come from a star
with four planets’? Or ‘Our planet
has four moons’? Or perhaps ‘We
come from the fourth planet of this
star, We come from Mars’.

T THREE In the morning on December 27, 1980, two
security patroimen on a routine tour of the
perimeter of the RAF/USAF airbase at Woodbridge
in Suffolk saw bright lights among the trees of the
adjacent Rendiesham Forest. Their first assumption
was that an aircraft might have overshot or misjudged the
runway and been forced into the trees, although they had
They asked permission to investigate
beyond the camp gates, which was duly granted.
In the forest It soon became clear that the object was no
aeroplane. They saw a metallic, triangular craft unlike anything

heard no nol:

they had seen before and
chased It through the trees.
And that was not the end of it.
Two ni tater, there was 8
similar sighting and a larger team

Investigate.

‘the military as light-ails, that had

light-alls refused to work.

the light saurce.

Weird.”

three objects in the sky,
except that they gave off red, green

through a power lens, but thelr

ning light-alls. A biue

“The MOD does look into reports of sightings that are sent to
us, many of which are very vague, but only to establish if
what was seen may have some defence significance, We believe
that down-to-earth explanations are available for most of
these reported sightings, such as aircraft seen from unusual
angles, or natural phenomena. If there is no evidence to
suggest a matter of defence relevance we do not investigate
or seek to establish the precise nature of what was observed.

Although the MOD did receive four reports for 2 Feb 96, none
were in the Morecambe or surrounding areas, as detailed in
your facsimile message. We are not aware of any matter of
defence concern associated with any of these sightings, or
the sightings near Morecombe which you have now brought to
our attention. Furthermore, no military aircraft were
authorised to operate at low level (below 2000 ft) in the
vicinity of Torrisholme at the time of the sighting."

As requested, attached please find brief lines on MOD involvement
in the subject of "UFOs".

Sec(AS)2al
MB8245 82140MB

IN REPORTS “UNIDENTIF “ OBJECT:

The MOD does not have any direct interest, expertise or role
with respect to "UFO/flying saucer" matters, or the question
of the existence or otherwise of extraterrestrial lifeforms,
about which we remain open-minded.

To date we are not aware of any evidence which substantiates
the existence of lifeforms of extraterrestrial origin.

The MOD does look into reports of "UFO" sightings that are
sent to us, many of which are very vague, but only to
establish if what was seen may have defence significance, eg.
hostile foreign military aircraft in UK airspace. We believe
that down-to-earth explanations are available for most
reported "UFO" sightings, such as aircraft seen from unusual
angles, or natural phenomena.

If there is no evidence to suggest a matter of defence
relevance we do not investigate or seek to establish the
precise nature of what was observed.

Secretariat (Air Staff), the single service secretariat
branch which provides administrative support for RAF
activities and operations, is the nominated MOD focal point
for the subject of "UFOs". Although there are two members of
staff in Sec(AS)2a who deal with “UFO"-related queries as a
part of their much wider ranging secretariat duties, there
are no staff who work on this topic full-time.

After 75 years, ,
market forces

attack the Naafi -

by ANDREW GILLIGAN jo

Defence Correspondent

SENIOR military officers
have attacked moves to com-
mercialise the Naafi and close
down many of its shops and
bars in Britain's garrisons.

The closures, now being
implemented, have been crit-
icised by one garrison com-
mander as “damaging a cor-
nerstone of Service life”.
Naafi, which celebrates its
75th anniversary this year,
has fed generations of
troops. Owned by Service-
men themselves as a form of
co-operative, its canteens are
art of every barracks and
post.

Now, however, a review
has recommended that con-
trol of Naafi should be.trans-
ferred to the organisation's
commercial managers. The
review, by Geoffrey Dart, a
Marks & Spencer director,
also proposed closing all but
the most isolated Naafi
shops in the UK. Mr Dart
takes over as Naafi's new
chief executive tomorrow,

“Defence cuts have
reduced our customer base
considerably and we have to
react to that,’’ said Rob
Howe, Naafi’s spokesman.
“We made a loss last year for
the first time in three-quar-
ters of a century.”

Only 15 of Naafi’s 100 Brit-
ish shops make significant
profits, with most others
breaking even. Naafi says
hat cuts on the mainland are
necessary to safeguard the
ssential, but loss-making

services in such high-risk
postings as Northern Ireland
and Bosnia. Most Service-
men based in Britain, they
say, now have easy access to
civilian shops.

“These days Servicemen
are much more mobile. They
can just jump in’ their cars
and go to Sainsbury's,” said
Mr Howe. “And where a
night out used to mean going
down the Naafi, these days
they get a cab and go to the
nightclub in town,”

Naafi managers also stress

UFO reports
shot down

Recent suggestions that the MOD has a high level of interest in
Unidentified Flying Objects were greeted with incredulity by the cell
actually tasked with overseeing this subject.

‘The two-man Secretariat(Air Staff)2a in Main Bi
‘existed to provide administrative for RAF activities and opera-
tions. UFOs were just one of a whole range of responsibilities.

‘They admitted receiving in the region of 200-300 reports of unex-
plained aerial sightings a year but denied anybody worked full time on
them.

The MOD did not have any direct interest, expertise or role in
respect of UFO matters, or those relating to the existence or otherwise
jof ial it about which it jined open minded. To
date, the MOD knew of no evidence which might substantiate the exis-
tence of such phenomena.

“With responsibility for the effective defence of the UK, the MOD
and HM Forces remain vigilant for any potential threat from whatever
source. It is in this context alone that reports of unexplained aerial
sightings are looked at in order to establish whether what was scen
might have defence significance.

“If no threat is dis sd — and in co with UFO
this has been the case in all instances to date ~ no further attempt is
made to investigate or establish exactly what might have been seen. As
such it is quite for a si to remain ined but not
require further official action.”

‘They said that despite the fact that only a small part of their work-
load was to act as a focal point for answering these type of queries,
they were often erroneously referred to as ‘the UFO office’.

“The majority of reports which we receive are from members of the
public who have witnessed something puzzling, usually lights in the
sky, and feet that they ought to inform the authorities of what they saw.
‘We look at these reports, most of which are very vague, to establish
“whether there is any defence interest.

“although we are able to provide a definitive i
of what the witness saw, for example we may already be aware of
unusual aerial activity which ties in with the sighting, we are not con-
stituted to investigate and provide an explanation for every unusual
sight in the sky.”

THERE was no evidence of
vampires in the lecture the-
, atre. No sign of aliens at the

one man was convinced he
had “‘smelled” an angel.

Most of the 1,500 people wha
attended the third annual
“Unconvention” of Fortean
Times, the magazine which
delves into the paranormal,
the unexplained and things
that go bump in the night,
wanted to believe in
something.

And there were plenty of
people to help them da it.
-David Heppeil, a curator at
the Royal Museum of Scot-
land, spoké on recent
reports of modern mer-
maids @iimin@mpeea former
UFO investigator for the
Ministry of Defence, him-
self a new believer, was
content to preach to the
converted.

Downstairs, Dr Richard
Wiseman, of the University
of Hertfordshire, a
researcher into paranormal
activity, was conducting
Victorian-style seances.

day Telegraph
1996

oly

“We're not trying to neip
People get in touch with the
dead.” he expiained, “But
we are interested in the
phenomena of seances;
what does occur and what
those who take part believe
has occurred are often very
different.”

There were lectures on the
Evolution of the Vampire,
the theme at the two-day
event; discussions of foot-
age showing the autopsies
of aliens; and a debate on

! What Are Ghosts? Inevita-
bly there was a hail full of
products to go with them.

David Lomax, the man who
smelled an angel, is a min-
ister of Greenhill New
Church in Barnet, Herts.

the presence last year. He
has now researched a book

life has been “different”
ever since.

of. Hertfordshire, who has
an animal science degree,
was interested in vampires.
“I find them fascinating
and erotic,”” she said.

* “Does that sound strange?
My mother thinks so.”

by the night’skies and her
search for UFOs, went so
far as to divorce her hus-
band because he did not
share her interests,

“T'd be out looking for UFOs
and studying the stars and
he only wanted to sit and
drink,” she said. “I think
that’s perfectly reasonable
grounds for divorce.”

omething spooky has tumed up

areply that confirmed the existence of

Authorizing Office?

MOD INTEREST IN “UFO" SIGHTINGS

fhe Ministry of Defence does not have any direct interest, expertise or
role in respect of "UFO/flying saucer" matters, or those relating to the
existence or otherwise of extraterrestrial lifeforms, about which we
remain open-minded. To date, however, the MOD knows of no evidence
which might substantiate the existence of such phenomena.

The MOD and HM Forces have responsibility for the effective defence of
the United Kingdom and we remain vigilant for any potential threat from
whatever source. It is in this context alone that we look at reports of
"unexplained" aerial sightings in order to establish whether what was
seen might have defence significance. If no threat is discerned, and in
connection with "UFO" sightings this has been the case in all instances
to date, we make no further attempt to investigate or establish exactly
what might have been seen. As such it is quite common for a sighting to
remain "unexplained" but not require further official action.

Secretariat (Air Staff), the single service secretariat which provides
administrative support for RAF activities and operations, based in MOD
Main Building London, is the nominated MOD focal point for the subject
of “UFOs". Although there are two members of staff in Sec(AS)2a who
deal with “UFO"-related queries as a part of their much wider ranging
duties, there are no staff which work on this topic full-time.

One of these staff members told us that "despite the fact that only a
small part of our workload is to act as focal point for answering
queries on the subject of "UFOs", we are often erroneously referred to
as 'The UFO office’.

“The majority of reports which we receive are from members of the
public who have witnessed something puzzling, usually lights in the sky,
and feel that they ought to inform the authorities of what they saw. We
look these reports, most of which are very vague, to establish whether
there is any defence interest.

“Although we are sometimes able to provide a definitive explanation
of what the witness saw, for example we may already be aware of unusual
aerial activity which ties in with the sighting, we are not constituted
to investigate and provide an explanation for every unusual sight in the
sky."

The MOD generally receives in the region of 200-300 reports of
"unexplained" aerial sightings per year.

MOD INTEREST IN "UFO" SIGHTINGS

The Ministry of Defence does not have any direct interest, expertise or
role in respect of "UFO/flying saucer" matters, or those relating to the
existence or otherwise of extraterrestrial lifeforms, about which we
remain open-minded. To date, however, the MOD knows of no evidence
which might substantiate the existence of such phenomena.

The MOD and HM Forces have responsibility for the effective defence of
the United Kingdom and we remain vigilant for any potential threat from
whatever source. It is in this context alone that we look at reports of
"unexplained" aerial sightings in order to establish whether what was
seen might have defence significance. If no threat is discerned, and in
connection with "UFO" sightings this has been the case in all instances
to date, we make no further attempt to investigate or establish exactly
what might have been seen. As such it is quite common for a sighting to
remain "unexplained" but not require further official action.

Secretariat (Air Staff), the single service secretariat which provides
administrative support for RAF activities and operations, is the
nominated focal point within the MOD for the subject of "UFOs".
However, there are no staff which work on this topic full-time and the
task represents a small part of the overall workload of Sec(AS)2.
Contrary to the widely held misapprehension by some members of the
public and the media, Sec(AS)2a is not a "UFO office" and neither is it
constituted to assist members of the public with finding explanations
for every seemingly inexplicable sight in the sky.

The MOD receives in the region of 200-300 reports of “unexplained"

aerial sightings a year.

information. The July 1993 White Paper on Open Government set out our policy ut this
ares. The Government agreed with the aims of freedom of Information, betieving that

In the White Paper we proposed a legislative approach in the following areas where there ate

. a stanitory right of accrss by individuals to their personal records, and

- a statutory right 10 health and safety information, a matter of vital interest both to
individuals and to the sommunity at large.

It is worth noting that these two areas account for the great majority of requests under other
countries’ freedom of information legislation. Legislation to enect these access tights will
be brought forward when Parliamentary time allows.

eo.
effective conduct of international negotiations, defence or government business generally.
Our view is that these are matters for Ministers and for Parliament (rather than for the

courts) and that @ direct liok with Ministerial accountability to Parliament should be
maintained.

The White Paper therefore proposed a Code of Practice on Access to Government
Information, which came into effect in April 1994. The Code of Practice includes the
following commitments:

i) to give facts and analysis with major policy decisions;
fi) to open up internal guidelines about departments’ dealings with the public;
1) to give reasons with administrative decisions:

iv) to provide information under the Citizen's Charter about public services. whet they
cost, targets, performance, complaints and redresa; and

vy) to answer requests for information.

Central to the Code of Practice is the enforcement role of the Parliamentary Ombudsman.
He provides an independent and external avenue of appeal in cases where Government
departments refuse to provide information. He reports to Parliament on his
tecommendations, thus providing the direct link between Ministerial accountability and
Parliament to which J referred above. Recourse to the Ombudsman has the further advantage
that, unlike enforcement through the courts, it 1s free to the applicant.

My department provides guidance and assistance to other government departments and public
bodies on implementing the Code of Practice. A report on the operation of the Code during
1995 is currently being prepared and will be published Jater this month, {1 is less than 1wo
years since the Code was introduced and it is cleasly too early to judge its full unpact.
Nonetheless, evidence so far suggests it is having a positive effect. We have been
encouraged by suggestions made to the PCA Select Committee during their -orcent enquiry
into open government that a change is occurring not only in the way in which deparements
respond to requests for information but also in the amount of information which is being
provided as a matter of course. This is a reflection of the Code's encouragemest of active
provision of information, a culture change which an Act would not necessarily bring about.

2h) mentions "the need for eleccoral reform." However, there are a number of
proposals for eiectoral reform, and does not say specifically which proposals
attract him. However, if this is a reference to proportional representation, the Government
has repeatedly affirmed that there are no plans to change the present electoral system at either
local oF national elections.

Proportional representation often results in some form of coalition government, at either
national or jocal ‘evel, based on a progranune which the electorate has not had the
upportunity to approve or disapprove. Whece it is used, general elections bave been known
to be followed by negotiations between the political parties involving deals and compromises
"to try to arrive at a solution for workable government, and from which the electorate are _

TO/SREE IM GIST. 96.

_ pe@"aOud ISection 40 | GSiGT 96, YOM 92
perae-fese 18:04 FROM 10 Section Bigha

ra . :

usually excluded and have no means of influencing. Furthermore, the various forms of

proportional representation each have particular disadvantages, so that there is no one system
which can safely be said to be the bes! solution. \

There is no perfect voting system. However, the Government remains of the view that the 1
simple majority system should be retained for local and parliamentary elections becausé it '
is welt understood by the electoraic, is more likely than alternative systems to produce local
authorities and governments which are directly accountable to the electorate and responsible

to them for the policies which they pursue, and that it contributes to stable government at

both national and local level.

With regard to a Bill of Rights, the Government believes that the rights of the individual in
this country are well safeguarded by our existing constitutional arrangements. in out view,
the introduction of a Bilt of Rights would represent a major change to these arrangements,
which have evolved over centuries and stood che test of time, for no advaitage.

Tn paticular, 4 Hill of Rights, and its possible entrenchment in a written constitution. would
alier the long-established constitutional balance in this country between Parliament, the
executive and the courts, whereby Parliament has primary responsibility for matters affecting °
the rights and liberties of the individual. Under such a Bill, the final decisions on such
matters would cest with judges, who are not directly accountable to the people of this
county, rather than with democratically elected Members of Parliament. fn the :
Government's view, this change is neither desirable nor appropriate.

The Government thinks that our unwritten constitution has served us well by provxiing the
system of cheeks and balances which is essential for the protection of democratic freedoms.
Gur constitutional arrangements have the flexibility to develop and adapt in accordance with
society's needs, through the amendment or enactment of statute Jaw, through jidicial
decisions and through the evolution of constitudonal conventions Soins of the most
repressive regimes in the world have a written coystitution: to have one is by no means a
guarantee of liberty,

It has tong been estabiishad in this country that a person is free to do as they wish unless the
law requires otherwise. We do not need to pass a Bill of Rights becanse izhts are
alteady inherent in the United Kingdom's legal systems, and ate protecte:! by there and by
Parliament, unless Parliament has decided ihat they should be restricted in some specific
way.

The Government attaches great importance to its obligations under the iuropesi Court of
Human Rights, and the other internaional human rights instruments to which it is a party.
In our view, however, incorporation of the ECHR into domestic law of the United Kingdom
ig neither neccessary nor desirable.

In the Goverament’s view incorporation would. like a Bill of Rights, strike at the
constitutional principle of the supremacy of Parliament. The areas of gublic policy covered
by the general principles sot out in the C vention have traditionally, and rightly, been the
province of Parliameat, rather than the courts, As things stand, it is for Parliamest to enact
detailed legislation on matters affecting the rights and liberties of the individual. In doing
